\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{SR}{(d_{1}\cdots d_{4})^{1-\varepsilon}}+RN_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}S^{4\varepsilon}.

We may now record our final estimate for (
5.19
).
Combining the previous line with (
5.21
) and (
5.22
),
and recalling
the choice of
N_{1}
made in (
5.20
),
we therefore deduce that

\displaystyle N_{{\mathbf{d}}}(S,R,Y)

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon,\theta}\frac{SR}{(d_{1}\cdots d_{4})^{1-\varepsilon}}+S+S^{1+\varepsilon}N_{1}^{\varepsilon}+RN_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}S^{4\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon,\theta}\frac{SR}{(d_{1}\cdots d_{4})^{1-\varepsilon}}+S^{1+2\varepsilon}+R(S^{3}R)^{\theta}S^{8\varepsilon}.

On rescaling
\varepsilon
, we finally obtain

(5.23)

N_{{\mathbf{d}}}(S,R,Y)\ll_{\varepsilon,\theta}\frac{SR}{(d_{1}\cdots d_{4})^{1-\varepsilon}}+S^{\varepsilon}\left(S+S^{3\theta}R^{1+\theta}\right),

for any
\theta>\frac{3}{16}
.

We are now ready to sum over all dyadic intervals in (
5.18
).
Inserting (
5.23
), it follows that

\displaystyle N_{{\mathbf{d}}}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon,\theta}\sum_{S\nearrow X}\sum_{R\nearrow S}\left(\frac{S^{\frac{5}{8}}Y^{\frac{4}{3}}}{R^{\frac{5}{8}}}+\frac{Y^{2}}{S^{\frac{1}{8}}R^{\frac{7}{8}}}\right)\left(\frac{SR}{(d_{1}\cdots d_{4})^{1-\varepsilon}}+S^{\varepsilon}\left(S+S^{3\theta}R^{1+\theta}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon,\theta}Y^{\frac{4}{3}}\left(\frac{X^{2}}{(d_{1}\cdots d_{4})^{1-\varepsilon}}+X^{\varepsilon}\left(X^{\frac{13}{8}}+X^{1+4\theta}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+Y^{2}\left(\frac{X}{(d_{1}\cdots d_{4})^{1-\varepsilon}}+X^{\varepsilon}\left(X^{\frac{7}{8}}+X^{4\theta}\right)\right).

Making the choice
\theta=\frac{7}{32}
, we obtain

N_{{\mathbf{d}}}(X,Y)\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{X^{2}Y^{\frac{4}{3}}+XY^{2}}{(d_{1}\cdots d_{4})^{1-\varepsilon}}+X^{\varepsilon}\left(X^{\frac{15}{8}}Y^{\frac{4}{3}}+X^{\frac{7}{8}}Y^{2}\right)

in (
5.17
).
It remains to sum over all
r,t
. Using the trivial bound for the divisor function
\tau_{4}
, we obtain

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,t\in{\mathbb{N}}\\
t~\square\text{-free},~t\ll X^{4}\\
r~\square\text{-full},~r\leqslant D\end{subarray}}\frac{r^{\frac{1}{4}+\varepsilon}}{t}\tau_{4}([r,t])\ll_{\varepsilon}X^{\varepsilon}D^{\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon}.

Similarly, in view of the lower bound

[r,t]\geqslant\max(r,t)\geqslant r^{\frac{7}{8}}t^{\frac{1}{8}}
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,t\in{\mathbb{N}}\\
t~\square\text{-free},~t\ll X^{4}\\
r~\square\text{-full},~r\leqslant D\end{subarray}}\frac{r^{\frac{1}{4}+\varepsilon}}{t}\frac{\tau_{4}([r,t])}{[r,t]^{1-\varepsilon}}\ll_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,t\in{\mathbb{N}}\\
t~\square\text{-free},~t\ll X^{4}\\
r~\square\text{-full},~r\leqslant D\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{r^{\frac{5}{8}-2\varepsilon}t^{\frac{9}{8}-2\varepsilon}}\ll_{\varepsilon}1.

The statement of Proposition
5.2
is now clear.
∎

5.2.
Proof of Proposition
5.4

Recall the definition (
5.11
) of
{\mathscr{S}}(X)
.
Then we have

\#{\mathscr{M}}(X,Y)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathscr{S}}(X)\end{subarray}}N(Q_{\mathbf{x}};Y),

where
Q_{\mathbf{x}}
is given by (
5.12
) and
N(Q_{\mathbf{x}};Y)
is the counting function defined in
(
4.1
).
We continue to adopt the notation
\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|
and
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})

that was introduced in (
5.16
).
In this section we see what can be deduced from an application of
Lemma
4.4
.
Letting
m(Q_{\mathbf{x}})=\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 4}|L_{i}(\mathbf{x})|
, we obtain

N(Q_{\mathbf{x}};Y)\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})^{\varepsilon}L(1,\chi_{Q_{\mathbf{x}}})}{(m(Q_{\mathbf{x}})\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}Y^{2}+\frac{\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|^{11+\varepsilon}}{m(Q_{\mathbf{x}})^{6}|\varDelta(\mathbf{x})|^{\frac{1}{2}}}Y^{\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon},

for any
\varepsilon>0
. Lemma
5.7
implies that
|\Delta(\mathbf{x})|^{\frac{1}{2}}\gg\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|^{\frac{3}{2}}m(Q_{\mathbf{x}})^{\frac{1}{2}}.
Since
\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|\ll X
, it follows that

(5.24)

\#{\mathscr{M}}(X,Y)\ll_{\varepsilon}Y^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathscr{S}}(X)\end{subarray}}\frac{\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})^{\varepsilon}L(1,\chi_{Q_{\mathbf{x}}})}{(m(Q_{\mathbf{x}})\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}+X^{\frac{19}{2}+\varepsilon}Y^{\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon}\hskip-5.69046pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathscr{S}}(X)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{m(Q_{\mathbf{x}})^{\frac{13}{2}}}.

But a standard dyadic decomposition procedure yields

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathscr{S}}(X)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{m(Q_{\mathbf{x}})^{\frac{13}{2}}}

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{4}\sum_{S\nearrow X}\frac{1}{S^{\frac{13}{2}}}\#\{\mathbf{x}\in{\mathscr{S}}(X):|L_{i_{1}}(\mathbf{x})|\sim S\}\ll X.

Thus the overall contribution from the second term is

\ll_{\varepsilon}X^{\frac{21}{2}+\varepsilon}Y^{\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon}\ll X^{11}Y^{\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon}.

In what follows we focus on the first summand. Much as in the proof of Proposition
5.2
, we carry out two dyadic decompositions. Then, for fixed
i_{1}\in\{1,\dots,4\}
, we are reduced to estimating

(5.25)

\sum_{S\nearrow X}\sum_{R\nearrow S}\frac{1}{S^{\frac{1}{2}}R^{\frac{1}{2}}}W_{i_{1}}(S,R),

where

(5.26)

W_{i_{1}}(S,R)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathscr{S}}(X)\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim S,~|L_{i_{1}}(\mathbf{x})|\sim R\end{subarray}}\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})^{\varepsilon}L(1,\chi_{Q_{\mathbf{x}}}).

One of the ingredients we will need in our treatment of

W_{i_{1}}(S,R)
is a proof that there are relatively few
\mathbf{x}
for which

\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})
is large. This is achieved in the following result.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\delta\geqslant 0
and let
i_{1}\in\{1,\dots,4\}
. Then

\#\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{2}:|\mathbf{x}|\sim S,~|L_{i_{1}}(\mathbf{x})|\sim R,~\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})>(SR)^{\delta}\right\}\ll(SR)^{1-\frac{\delta}{8}}.

Proof.

Let
N_{\delta}(S,R)
denote the quantity that is to be estimated. First,
we observe that the condition
\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})>(SR)^{\delta}
implies that at least one of
L_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\dots,L_{4}(\mathbf{x})
has a square-full part that exceeds

(SR)^{\frac{\delta}{4}}
. Let us assume that
L_{i_{0}}
has square-full part
>(SR)^{\frac{\delta}{4}}
, with
i_{0}\neq i_{1}
. Upon a non-singular change of variables, we may therefore assume that
L_{i_{0}}(\mathbf{x})=x_{1}
and
L_{i_{1}}(\mathbf{x})=x_{2}
. But then, on summing trivially over
x_{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle N_{\delta}(S,R)

\displaystyle\ll R\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(SR)^{\frac{\delta}{4}}\ll a\ll S\\
a~\square\text{-full}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\neq 0}\\
|x_{1}|\leqslant S,~a\mid x_{1}\end{subarray}}1\ll(SR)^{1-\frac{\delta}{8}},

which is satisfactory. Alternatively, if
L_{i_{1}}(\mathbf{x})

is the term with large square-full part, then a similar manipulation yields

\displaystyle N_{\delta}(S,R)

\displaystyle\ll S\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(SR)^{\frac{\delta}{4}}\ll a\ll R\\
a~\square\text{-full}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\neq 0}\\
|x_{1}|\leqslant R,~a\mid x_{1}\end{subarray}}1\ll(SR)^{1-\frac{\delta}{8}},

which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We are now ready to estimate the quantity in
(
5.26
).

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\delta\geqslant 0
. Then

W_{i_{1}}(S,R)\ll_{\varepsilon}SR+S^{\varepsilon}\left((SR)^{\frac{\delta}{2}}\left(S+S^{\frac{21}{32}}R^{\frac{39}{32}}\right)+(SR)^{1-\frac{\delta}{8}}\right).

Proof.

For given
\delta\geqslant 0
, we write

W_{i_{1}}(S,R)=W_{i_{1},1}(S,R;\delta)+W_{i_{1},2}(S,R;\delta),

where the sum
W_{i_{1},1}(S,R;\delta)
is subject to the condition
\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})\leqslant(SR)^{\delta}
and
W_{i_{1},2}(S,R;\delta)
has
\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})>(SR)^{\delta}
.

Observe that

(5.27)

\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})^{\varepsilon}L(1,\chi_{Q_{\mathbf{x}}})\ll|\mathbf{x}|^{4\varepsilon}\log\left(2+\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|\right)\ll_{\varepsilon}X^{5\varepsilon}.

Rescaling
\varepsilon
and applying Lemma
5.9
, we get a satisfactory bound
for the term
W_{i_{1},2}(S,R;\delta)
.
Turning to

W_{i_{1},1}(S,R;\delta)
, we begin in the same way as in the proof of
Proposition
5.2
. Thus

\displaystyle W_{i_{1},1}(S,R;\delta)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\leqslant(SR)^{\delta}\\
d~\square\text{-full}\end{subarray}}d^{\varepsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathbb{N}^{4}\\
d=d_{1}\cdots d_{4}\end{subarray}}N^{\prime}_{i_{1},{\mathbf{d}}}(S,R),

where

N^{\prime}_{i_{1},{\mathbf{d}}}(S,R)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathscr{S}}(X)\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim S,~|L_{i_{1}}(\mathbf{x})|\sim R\\
d_{i}\mid L_{i}(\mathbf{x})\end{subarray}}L(1,\chi_{Q_{\mathbf{x}}}).

This is essentially the same sum
that we already met in (
5.19
) and we can directly apply the bound (
5.23
).
Taking
\theta=\frac{7}{32}
, we get

N^{\prime}_{i_{1},{\mathbf{d}}}(S,R)\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{SR}{(d_{1}\cdots d_{4})^{1-\varepsilon}}+S^{\varepsilon}\left(S+S^{\frac{21}{32}}R^{\frac{39}{32}}\right),

whence

W_{i_{1},1}(S,R;\delta)\ll_{\varepsilon}SR+S^{\varepsilon}(SR)^{\frac{\delta}{2}}(S+S^{\frac{21}{32}}R^{\frac{39}{32}}).

∎

Inserting Lemma
5.10
into (
5.25
) and
summing over dyadic intervals, we are now led to the conclusion that

\sum_{S\nearrow X}\sum_{R\nearrow S}\frac{1}{S^{\frac{1}{2}}R^{\frac{1}{2}}}W_{i_{1}}(S,R)\ll_{\varepsilon}X+X^{\varepsilon}\left(X^{\frac{7}{8}+\delta}+X^{1-\frac{\delta}{4}}\right).

Taking
\delta=\frac{1}{10}
, the right hand side is
O(X)
, on taking
\varepsilon
sufficiently small. Therefore the overall contribution of the first term in
(
5.24
)
is
O(XY^{2})
, thereby completing the proof of Proposition
5.2
.

5.3.
Proof of Proposition
5.6

Our proof of Proposition
5.6
relies on viewing the equation (
1.1
) in the form
(
1.3
), where
Q_{1},Q_{2}
are the diagonal quadratic forms defined in
(
2.2
), where
\gcd(a_{i},b_{i})=1
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 4
. As usual, we proceed under the assumption that the pencil
Q_{1}=Q_{2}=0
defines a smooth curve
Z\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}

of genus
1
,
such that
Z(\mathbb{R})=\emptyset
.

We will be led to make crucial use of properties of the lattice
\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],k}
that was defined in (
3.9
). In particular, we will need to show that its successive minima are not typically
lop-sided.
We begin, however, with the following basic estimate.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
D,E>0
such that
1\ll E\ll D^{3/4}.
Then

\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{v}|\leqslant E\\
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{v}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1\ll E^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{E^{4}}{D}\cdot\frac{\log E}{\log\log E}.

Proof.

If the left hand side is non-zero, then there exists a vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
such that

|\mathbf{v}|\leqslant E
and
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{v})
for
i=1,2
. But then

D\ll E^{2}
, since

Q_{1}(\mathbf{v}),Q_{2}(\mathbf{v})
cannot both vanish. Thus we may proceed under the assumption that
D\ll E^{2}
.

We sort the sum on the left according to the value of
\gcd(Q_{1}(\mathbf{v}),Q_{2}(\mathbf{v}))
. This gives

\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}|\leqslant E\\
d|Q_{i}(\mathbf{v})\text{ for }i=1,2\end{subarray}}1=\sum_{c=0}^{\log(ME^{2}/D)}\sum_{g\sim 2^{c}D}\tau_{D}(g)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}|\leqslant E\\
g=\gcd(Q_{1}(\mathbf{v}),Q_{2}(\mathbf{v}))\end{subarray}}1,

where
\tau_{D}(g)=\#\{d\asymp D:d\mid g\}
and

M=\max\left(|a_{1}|+\cdots+|a_{4}|,|b_{1}|+\cdots+|b_{4}|\right).

We claim that

\tau_{D}(g)\ll\min\left(2^{c},\operatorname{e}^{\frac{2\log E}{\log\log E}}\right).

Indeed, if
g\in[2^{c}D,2^{c+1}D)
and
g=fd
for some
d\asymp D
, then
2^{c}\ll f\leqslant 2^{c}
. This implies that
\tau_{D}(g)\ll 2^{c}
. On the other hand,

\tau_{D}(g)\leqslant\tau(g)\leqslant\operatorname{e}^{(\log 2+o(1))\frac{\log g}{\log\log g}}\ll\operatorname{e}^{\frac{\log g}{\log\log g}},

by Tenenbaum
[
27
, Thm. 5.4]
, for example. The claim follows, since
g\leqslant ME^{2}
.

Next, we observe that if
g=\gcd(Q_{1}(\mathbf{v}),Q_{2}(\mathbf{v}))
then

\frac{Q_{1}(\mathbf{v})}{g}\cdot Q_{2}(\mathbf{v})=Q_{1}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\frac{Q_{2}(\mathbf{v})}{g},

where
\gcd(g^{-1}Q_{1}(\mathbf{v}),g^{-1}Q_{2}(\mathbf{v}))=1
. Moreover, we also have

\max\left(\frac{|Q_{1}(\mathbf{v})|}{g},\frac{|Q_{2}(\mathbf{v})|}{g}\right)\leqslant\frac{ME^{2}}{2^{c}D}.

It follows that

(5.28)

\sum_{g\sim 2^{c}D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}|\leqslant E\\
g=\gcd(Q_{1}(\mathbf{v}),Q_{2}(\mathbf{v}))\end{subarray}}1\leqslant\#{\mathscr{M}}^{*}\left(\frac{ME^{2}}{2^{c}D},E\right),

in the notation of (
5.4
).
Hence Corollary
5.5
yields

(5.29)

\begin{split}\#{\mathscr{M}}^{*}\left(\frac{ME^{2}}{2^{c}D},E\right)&\ll_{\varepsilon}E^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{E^{4}}{2^{c}D}+\frac{E^{16/3}}{4^{c}D^{2}},\end{split}

for any
\varepsilon>0
,
provided that

ME^{2}/(2^{c}D)\leqslant E^{2/3}\log E.

The latter is equivalent to
ME^{4/3}\leqslant 2^{c}D\log E
, which is implied by the hypothesis of the lemma.

We shall argue differently according to the size of
c
. Let
L>0
be a parameter to be selected in due course. For small
c
, it follows from (
5.28
) and (
5.29
) that

\displaystyle\sum_{c=0}^{L}\sum_{g\sim 2^{c}D}\tau_{D}(g)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}|\leqslant E\\
g=\gcd(Q_{1}(\mathbf{v}),Q_{2}(\mathbf{v}))\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}\sum_{c=0}^{L}2^{c}\left(E^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{E^{4}}{2^{c}D}+\frac{E^{16/3}}{4^{c}D^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}2^{L}E^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{LE^{4}}{D}+\frac{E^{16/3}}{D^{2}}.

On the other hand, for the remaining
c
, the (
5.28
) and (
5.29
) yield

\displaystyle\sum_{c>L}\sum_{g\sim 2^{c}D}\tau_{D}(g)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}|\leqslant E\\
g=\gcd(Q_{1}(\mathbf{v}),Q_{2}(\mathbf{v}))\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}\operatorname{e}^{\frac{2\log E}{\log\log E}}\sum_{c>L}\left(E^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{E^{4}}{2^{c}D}+\frac{E^{16/3}}{4^{c}D^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\operatorname{e}^{\frac{2\log E}{\log\log E}}\left(E^{2+\varepsilon}\log E+\frac{E^{4}}{2^{L}D}+\frac{E^{16/3}}{4^{L}D^{2}}\right).

We shall take

L=\frac{4\log E}{\log\log E}
.
It now follows that

\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}|\leqslant E\\
d|Q_{i}(\mathbf{v})\text{ for }i=1,2\end{subarray}}1\ll_{\varepsilon}E^{2+2\varepsilon}+\frac{E^{4}}{D}\cdot\frac{\log E}{\log\log E}+\frac{E^{16/3}}{D^{2}},

since

c^{L}\log E=O_{\varepsilon}(E^{\varepsilon})
for any
c\geqslant 1
. Now
E^{16/3}/D^{2}\leqslant E^{4}/D
if
E\leqslant D^{3/4}
. Hence the lemma follows on redefining the choice of
\varepsilon
.
∎

We can use this result to assess the average size of the smallest successive minimum

s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],k}
of the lattice
lattice
\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],k}
that was defined in (
3.9
), for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and

[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{k}^{\times}
, in the notation of (
3.8
).
At this point it is convenient to recall the notation (
3.11
), where
\Delta

is the product of bad primes defined in (
2.1
). The following result is rather general, since it will be used in more than one context in what follows.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
f,e,m,D\in\mathbb{N}
and assume that
m\mid e
. Then

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\asymp D\\
f|d\end{subarray}}\sum_{[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}}\frac{1}{s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{mf}}^{3}}\ll m_{\Delta}f_{\Delta}\cdot(mf)^{\frac{5}{4}}\cdot(De)^{-\frac{1}{4}}\frac{\log De}{\log\log De}.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
be a non-zero vector in the lattice

\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{mf}}
, with Euclidean length equal to

s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{mf}}
. This implies that

\frac{de}{mf}\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{v})
for
i=1,2
.
Since we cannot have
Q_{1}(\mathbf{v})=Q_{2}(\mathbf{v})=0
, so it follows that

\frac{de}{mf}\ll|\mathbf{v}|^{2}
. Once combined with (
3.10
), this therefore implies that the smallest successive minimum of

\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{mf}}
satisfies

\left(\frac{de}{mf}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{mf}}\ll\left(\frac{de}{mf}\right)^{\frac{3}{4}}.

Splitting the sum in the lemma into dyadic intervals, we obtain

(5.30)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\asymp D\\
f|d\end{subarray}}\sum_{[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}}\frac{1}{s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{mf}}^{3}}\ll\sum_{\left(\frac{De}{mf}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\swarrow E\nearrow\left(\frac{De}{mf}\right)^{\frac{3}{4}}}\frac{S(E)}{E^{3}},

where

\displaystyle S(E)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\asymp D\\
f|d\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}\\
s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{mf}}\sim E\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{v}|\sim E\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\asymp D\\
f|d\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}\\
\mathbf{v}\in\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{mf}}\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{d^{\prime}\asymp\frac{D}{f}}\sum_{\ell\mid\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}|\sim E\\
\gcd(\mathbf{v},\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m})=\ell\\
\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{v}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}\text{}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{d^{\prime}ef}^{\times}\\
\mathbf{v}\in\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}}\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle=\sum_{d^{\prime}\asymp\frac{D}{f}}\sum_{\ell|\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}|\sim E/\ell\\
\gcd(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m})=1\\
\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}\mid Q_{i}(\ell\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}\text{}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{d^{\prime}ef}^{\times}\\
\ell\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}}\end{subarray}}1.

We observe that if
\ell\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}}
, with
\ell\mid\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}
and
\gcd(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m})=1
, then there exists
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\ell\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\equiv\lambda\cdot\mathbf{u}\bmod{\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}}
, which implies that
\lambda=\ell\lambda^{\prime}
for some
\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
which is coprime with
\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m}
. But then it follows that

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\equiv\lambda^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{u}\bmod{\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m}}
and so
Q_{i}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\equiv 0\bmod{\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m}}
, for
i=1,2
. Hence

S(E)\leqslant\sum_{d^{\prime}\asymp\frac{D}{f}}\sum_{\ell|\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}|\sim E/\ell\\
\gcd(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m})=1\\
\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m}\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{d^{\prime}ef}^{\times}\\
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m}}\end{subarray}}1.

The first part of
Lemma
3.5
implies that the inner sum is
O((\ell_{\Delta}m_{\Delta}f_{\Delta})\cdot(\ell mf))
.
If write
M_{m,f}=m_{\Delta}f_{\Delta}mf
, then it follows that

\begin{split}S(E)&\ll M_{m,f}\sum_{d^{\prime}\asymp\frac{D}{f}}\sum_{\ell|\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}|\sim E/\ell\\
\gcd(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m})=1\\
\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m}\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}\ell_{\Delta}\ell\ll M_{m,f}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}\\
p\mid k\Rightarrow p\mid\Delta\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\ll E\\
\ell_{\Delta}=k\end{subarray}}k\ell\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\asymp\frac{D}{f}\\
\ell|\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}|\sim E/\ell\\
\gcd(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m})=1\\
\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m}\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1.\end{split}

It now follows from
Lemma
5.11
that
the inner sum is

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\asymp\frac{D}{f}\\
\ell|\frac{d^{\prime}e}{m}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}|\sim E/\ell\\
\gcd(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m})=1\\
\frac{d^{\prime}e}{\ell m}\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime\prime}\asymp\frac{De}{\ell mf}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}|\sim E/\ell\\
d^{\prime\prime}\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1\ll_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{E}{\ell}\right)^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{mfE^{4}}{\ell^{3}De}\cdot\frac{\log E}{\log\log E},

for any
\varepsilon>0
,
since clearly

\frac{E}{\ell}\ll\left(\frac{De}{\ell mf}\right)^{3/4}

in (
5.30
).
Thus

S(E)\ll_{\varepsilon}M_{m,f}\left(E^{2+\varepsilon}U(1+\varepsilon)+\frac{mfE^{4}}{De}\cdot\frac{\log E}{\log\log E}\cdot U(2)\right),

where

U(\theta)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}\\
p\mid k\Rightarrow p\mid\Delta\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\ll E\\
\ell_{\Delta}=k\end{subarray}}\frac{k}{\ell^{\theta}}.

Finally, for any
\theta>1
, we note that

U(\theta)\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\ll E\\
p\mid k\Rightarrow p\mid\Delta\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell^{\prime}\ll E\end{subarray}}\frac{k^{1-\theta}}{{\ell^{\prime}}^{\theta}}\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\ll E\\
p\mid k\Rightarrow p\mid\Delta\end{subarray}}k^{1-\theta}\ll_{\theta}1.

Hence

S(E)\ll_{\varepsilon}M_{m,f}\left(E^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{mfE^{4}}{De}\cdot\frac{\log E}{\log\log E}\right).

Returning to (
5.30
) and summing over dyadic intervals for
E
, the statement of the lemma easily follows.
∎

Armed with the previous facts about our lattices
\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],k}
,
we are now ready to establish the following result, which is a critical step towards
Proposition
5.6
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
, let
D\geqslant 1
and let
e\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{y}|\leqslant Y\\
de|Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{Y^{4}}{De^{2-\varepsilon}}+\frac{Y^{3}}{(De)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\frac{\log De}{\log\log De}+D^{2}e^{1+\varepsilon}.

Proof.

Dropping the primitivity condition, we first note that

\begin{split}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{y}|\leqslant Y\\
de|Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1&\leqslant\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{y}|\leqslant Y\\
\mathbf{y}\in\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],de}\end{subarray}}1,\end{split}

where the inner sum is now over all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
. Recalling that
\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],de}
is an integer lattice of rank
4
and determinant
(de)^{3}
, it follows from a lattice point counting result due to Schmidt

[
23
, Lemma
2
]
that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{y}|\leqslant Y\\
\mathbf{y}\in\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],de}\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\ll\frac{Y^{4}}{(de)^{3}}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{Y^{j}}{s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],de}\cdots s_{j,[\mathbf{u}],de}}+1\ll\frac{Y^{4}}{(de)^{3}}+\frac{Y^{3}}{s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],de}^{3}}+1,

where
1\leqslant s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],de}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant s_{4,[\mathbf{u}],de}
are the successive minima of
\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],de}
.

Taking
f=m=1
in
Lemma
5.12
, we obtain

\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}}\frac{Y^{3}}{s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],de}^{3}}\ll\frac{Y^{3}}{(De)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\frac{\log De}{\log\log De}.

Moreover,

\begin{split}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}}\left(\frac{Y^{4}}{(de)^{3}}+1\right)\ll\left(Y^{4}+D^{3}e^{3}\right)\sum_{d\asymp D}\frac{\#V_{de}^{\times}}{(de)^{3}}.\end{split}

To estimate
\#V_{de}^{\times}
we may appeal to the second part of Lemma
3.5
, which
gives

\displaystyle\#V_{de}^{\times}

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}d_{\Delta}^{\varepsilon}e^{1+\varepsilon}\cdot d\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p|d\\
p\nmid\Delta\end{subarray}}\left(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})\right),

for any
\varepsilon>0
. Hence

\sum_{d\asymp D}\frac{\#V_{de}^{\times}}{(de)^{3}}\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{e^{2-\varepsilon}}\sum_{d\asymp D}\frac{d_{\Delta}^{\varepsilon}}{d^{2}}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid d\\
p\nmid\Delta\end{subarray}}\left(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})\right)\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{De^{2-\varepsilon}}.

The statement of the lemma follows on collecting together the various estimates.
∎

Combing the latter with our earlier work, we can now record the following bound.

Corollary 5.14
.

Let
D,Y\geqslant 1
. Then

\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{y}|\leqslant Y\\
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1\ll_{\varepsilon}Y^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{Y^{4}}{D}\log YD,

for any
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

If
D\ll Y^{\frac{4}{3}}
, then we apply Lemma
5.13
with
e=1
. Otherwise,
if
D\gg Y^{\frac{4}{3}}
,
the desired bound is a consequence of
Lemma
5.11
.
This completes the proof.
∎

We now have all the tools in place to complete the proof of
Proposition
5.6
.
On appealing to Lemma
5.1
and
breaking the range of
|\mathbf{y}|
into dyadic intervals,
we find that

\#{\mathscr{M}}(X,Y)\ll_{\varepsilon}X^{\varepsilon}Y^{2+\varepsilon}+\sum_{Y_{0}\nearrow Y}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y_{0}\end{subarray}}M(X,\mathbf{y}),

for any
\varepsilon>0
,
where

M(X,\mathbf{y})=\#\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{2}:\text{\eqref{eq:xQ} holds},~|\mathbf{x}|\sim X\right\}.

Since
\gcd(x_{1},x_{2})=1
, (
1.3
) implies that

x_{1}=\pm Q_{2}(\mathbf{y})/d
and
x_{2}=\pm Q_{1}(\mathbf{y})/d
,
where
d=\gcd(Q_{1}(\mathbf{y}),Q_{2}(\mathbf{y}))
. In particular, we must have

\max(|Q_{1}(\mathbf{y})|,|Q_{2}(\mathbf{y})|)\sim Xd
.
Let

C=\inf_{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{4},|\mathbf{t}|\sim 1}\max\left(|Q_{1}(\mathbf{t})|,|Q_{2}(\mathbf{t})|\right).

Our assumption that
Z(\mathbb{R})=\emptyset
implies that
C>0
, for a constant
C
that depends only on the coefficients of
Q_{1},Q_{2}.
It follows from homogeneity that

\max(|Q_{1}(\mathbf{y})|,|Q_{2}(\mathbf{y})|)\asymp Y_{0}^{2},

for any
|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y_{0}
.
In this way we deduce that

\#{\mathscr{M}}(X,Y)\ll_{\varepsilon}X^{\varepsilon}Y^{2+\varepsilon}+\sum_{Y_{0}\nearrow Y}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y_{0}\\
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1,

for any
\varepsilon>0
,
where
D=Y_{0}^{2}/X
.
We now apply
Lemma
5.13
, which gives

\displaystyle\#{\mathscr{M}}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}X^{\varepsilon}Y^{2+\varepsilon}+\sum_{Y_{0}\nearrow Y}\left(\frac{Y_{0}^{4}}{D}+\frac{Y_{0}^{3}}{D^{\frac{1}{4}}}\frac{\log D}{\log\log D}+D^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=X^{\varepsilon}Y^{2+\varepsilon}\sum_{Y_{0}\nearrow Y}\left(XY_{0}^{2}+X^{\frac{1}{4}}Y_{0}^{\frac{5}{2}}\frac{\log Y_{0}}{\log\log Y_{0}}+\frac{Y_{0}^{4}}{X^{2}}\right),

on taking
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{4}
and
D=Y_{0}^{2}/X
. Proposition
5.6
readily follows on summing over
Y_{0}
.

6.
Asymptotics via the geometry of numbers

The purpose of this section is to prove Proposition
2.2
, which provides an asymptotic formula for

\#{\mathscr{L}}_{1}(B)=\#\left\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in{\mathscr{L}}(B):B^{\frac{1}{4}+\eta}\leqslant|\mathbf{x}|\leqslant B^{\frac{1}{2}-\eta}\right\},

where
{\mathscr{L}}(B)
is given by (
2.6
).
In particular, we note that any
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
counted by

\#{\mathscr{L}}_{1}(B)
must satisfy

|\mathbf{y}|\leqslant B^{\frac{3}{8}-\frac{\eta}{2}}
. Breaking into dyadic intervals, we therefore find that

\#{\mathscr{L}}_{1}(B)=\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{Y\nearrow B^{\frac{3}{8}-\frac{\eta}{2}}}\hskip-8.5359pt\#\left\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{4}:\begin{array}[]{l}\text{\eqref{eq:quadbundle} and \eqref{eq:notsquare} hold},~|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y\\
B^{\frac{1}{4}+\eta}\leqslant|\mathbf{x}|\leqslant\min(B/|\mathbf{y}|^{2},B^{\frac{1}{2}-\eta})\end{array}\right\}.

We claim that there is a satisfactory overall contribution from
Y

in the range
Y\leqslant B^{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\eta}{2}}.

But it follows from Theorem

2.1
that this contribution is

\ll\sum_{Y\nearrow B^{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\eta}{2}}}\left(B^{\frac{1}{2}-\eta}\cdot Y^{2}+\left(\frac{B}{Y^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}Y^{\frac{5}{2}}\frac{\log B}{\log\log B}\right)\ll B,

on breaking the sum over
\mathbf{x}
into dyadic intervals.
We can use Lemma
5.1
to handle the overall contribution from those
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
for which
(
2.3
) fails. Hence

(6.1)

\#{\mathscr{L}}_{1}(B)=\sum_{B^{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\eta}{2}}\swarrow Y\nearrow B^{\frac{3}{8}-\frac{\eta}{2}}}\#\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(Y)+O(B),

where

(6.2)

\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(Y)=\left\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{4}:\begin{array}[]{l}\text{\eqref{eq:quadbundle} holds},~|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y\\
B^{\frac{1}{4}+\eta}\leqslant|\mathbf{x}|\leqslant B/|\mathbf{y}|^{2}\end{array}\right\}.

The main goal of this section is to produce the following asymptotic formula for the cardinality of

\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(Y)
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
Y\geqslant 1
such that
B^{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\eta}{2}}\ll Y\ll B^{\frac{3}{8}-\frac{\eta}{2}}
.
Then

\#\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(Y)=2\mathfrak{S}_{1}B\left(\sigma_{\infty}(Y)+O\left(\frac{Y^{2}}{B^{\frac{3}{4}-\eta}}\right)\right)+O\left(\frac{B}{\sqrt{\log B}}\right),

where

\mathfrak{S}_{1}
is given by (
2.8
) and

(6.3)

\sigma_{\infty}(Y)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}}{|\mathbf{y}|^{2}\max(|Q_{1}(\mathbf{y})|,|Q_{2}(\mathbf{y})|)}.

We may insert this result into (
6.1
), noting that

\sum_{Y\nearrow B^{\frac{3}{8}-\frac{\eta}{2}}}Y^{2}/B^{\frac{3}{4}-\eta}\ll 1.

Hence we obtain

\#{\mathscr{L}}_{1}(B)=2\mathfrak{S}_{1}B\cdot\sum_{B^{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\eta}{2}}\swarrow Y\nearrow B^{\frac{3}{8}-\frac{\eta}{2}}}\sigma_{\infty}(Y)+O\left(B\sqrt{\log B}\right).

This therefore completes the proof of Proposition
2.2
, subject to
Proposition
6.1
.

6.1.
Preliminary steps

We now turn to the task of estimating the cardinality of
(
6.2
).
Rewriting (
1.1
) as (
1.3
) and extracting the greatest common divisor
d
of
Q_{1}(\mathbf{y})
and
Q_{2}(\mathbf{y})
, we begin our proof of Proposition
6.1

by writing

\#\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(Y)=\sum_{d=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
d=\gcd(Q_{1}(\mathbf{y}),Q_{2}(\mathbf{y}))\end{subarray}}2,

where the factor
2
corresponds to the possible parameterisations

(x_{1},x_{2})=d^{-1}(Q_{2}(\mathbf{y}),Q_{1}(\mathbf{y}))
and
(x_{1},x_{2})=-d^{-1}(Q_{2}(\mathbf{y}),Q_{1}(\mathbf{y}))
,
and we have put

(6.4)

\mathscr{B}(Y,d)=\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{\mathbb{R}}^{4}:|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y,~B^{\frac{1}{4}+\eta}\leqslant\frac{\max(|Q_{1}(\mathbf{y})|,|Q_{2}(\mathbf{y})|)}{d}\leqslant\frac{B}{|\mathbf{y}|^{2}}\right\}.

The assumption
Z(\mathbb{R})=\emptyset
implies that
\max(|Q_{1}(\mathbf{y})|,|Q_{2}(\mathbf{y})|)\asymp Y^{2}
for any

\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
such that

|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y
.
Hence

D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}

where

(6.5)

D_{1}=\frac{Y^{4}}{B}\quad\text{ and }\quad D_{2}=\frac{Y^{2}}{B^{\frac{1}{4}+\eta}}.

It follows that

\#\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(Y)=\sum_{D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
d=\gcd(Q_{1}(\mathbf{y}),Q_{2}(\mathbf{y}))\end{subarray}}2.

Let
M,z>0
be parameters to be chosen in due course and define
P(z)=\prod_{p\leqslant z}p.

Let

(6.6)

T_{1}=\sum_{D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\\
\gcd(d^{-1}Q_{1}(\mathbf{y}),d^{-1}Q_{2}(\mathbf{y}),P(z))=1\end{subarray}}1.

Then
we clearly have

(6.7)

2T_{1}-T_{2}-T_{3}\leqslant\#\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(X,Y)\leqslant 2T_{1},

where

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\\
\exists p\in(z,M]\text{ s.t. }p\mid\frac{Q_{i}(\mathbf{y})}{d},~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1,\qquad T_{3}=\sum_{D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\\
\exists p>M\text{ s.t. }p\mid\frac{Q_{i}(\mathbf{y})}{d},~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1.

We shall produce upper bounds for
T_{2}
and
T_{3}
, and an asymptotic formula for
T_{1}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Then

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\ll_{\eta}\frac{B}{z^{1-\varepsilon}}+B^{1-\frac{\eta}{2}}M^{3}\quad\text{ and }\quad T_{3}\ll_{\varepsilon}Y^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{B(\log B)^{2}}{M}.

Proof.

We start by noting that
T_{2}\leqslant\sum_{p\in(z,M]}U_{p}
, where

U_{p}=\sum_{D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y\\
dp\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1,

for any prime
p
.
We may use
Lemma
5.13
to estimate
U_{p}
,
on breaking the
d
-sum into dyadic intervals. In this way, on recalling (
6.5
), we see that

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}\sum_{p\in(z,M]}\left(\frac{Y^{4}}{D_{1}p^{2-\varepsilon}}+\frac{Y^{3}}{(D_{1}p)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\frac{\log D_{2}p}{\log\log D_{2}p}+D_{2}^{2}p^{1+\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{Y^{4}}{D_{1}z^{1-\varepsilon}}+\frac{Y^{3}M^{\frac{3}{4}}\log B}{D_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}}}+D_{2}^{2}M^{2+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{B}{z^{1-\varepsilon}}+B^{\frac{1}{4}}Y^{2}M^{\frac{3}{4}}\log B+\frac{Y^{4}M^{2+\varepsilon}}{B^{\frac{1}{2}+2\eta}},

for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Since
Y\ll B^{\frac{3}{8}-\frac{\eta}{2}}
, it follows that

B^{\frac{1}{4}}Y^{2}M^{\frac{3}{4}}\log B\ll B^{1-\eta}M^{\frac{3}{4}}\log B\ll_{\eta}B^{1-\frac{\eta}{2}}M^{3}.

Similarly,

\frac{Y^{4}M^{2+\varepsilon}}{B^{\frac{1}{2}+2\eta}}\ll B^{1-4\eta}M^{2+\varepsilon}\ll B^{1-\frac{\eta}{2}}M^{3},

which completes the proof of the first part of the lemma.

We now turn to

T_{3}\leqslant\sum_{p>M}U_{p}.

If
pd\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y})
for
i=1,2
then
there exists
q\gg D_{1}M
such that
q\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y})
for
i=1,2
.
Under our assumption
Z(\mathbb{R})=\emptyset
, it follows that

T_{3}\ll\log Y\sum_{D_{1}M\ll q\ll Y^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y\\
q\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1,

since there
O(\log Y)
primes divisors of
q
.
Splitting the range of summation over
q
into dyadic intervals,
Corollary
5.14
yields

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle\ll\log Y\sum_{D_{1}M\swarrow G\nearrow Y^{2}}\left(Y^{2+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}+\frac{Y^{4}}{G}\log B\right)\ll_{\varepsilon}Y^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{Y^{4}}{D_{1}M}(\log B)^{2}.

Recalling (
6.5
),
this is also satisfactory for the lemma.
∎

6.2.
Asymptotic formula for
T_{1}

It remains to deal with the sum (
6.6
).
The first step is to reduce the primitivity condition on
\mathbf{y}
to the requirement that
\gcd(\mathbf{y},dP(z))=1
. This is the purpose of the following result.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Then

T_{1}=\Sigma_{1}+O_{\varepsilon}\left(Y^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{B\log B}{z^{3}}\right),

where

\Sigma_{1}=\sum_{D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
\gcd(\mathbf{y},dP(z))=1\\
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\\
\gcd(d^{-1}Q_{1}(\mathbf{y}),d^{-1}Q_{2}(\mathbf{y}),P(z))=1\end{subarray}}\hskip-14.22636pt1.

Proof.

The proof hinges on the observation that

T_{1}-\sum_{D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
\gcd(\mathbf{y},dP(z))=1\\
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\\
\gcd(d^{-1}Q_{1}(\mathbf{y}),d^{-1}Q_{2}(\mathbf{y}),P(z))=1\end{subarray}}\hskip-14.22636pt1\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k>1\\
\gcd(k,P(z))=1\end{subarray}}R_{k},

where now

R_{k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\\
\gcd(d,k)=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
k=\gcd(y_{1},\dots,y_{4})\\
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\\
\gcd(d^{-1}Q_{1}(\mathbf{y}),d^{-1}Q_{2}(\mathbf{y}),P(z))=1\end{subarray}}\hskip-14.22636pt1.

But clearly

\displaystyle R_{k}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\\
\gcd(d,k)=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\cap k^{-1}\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),~i=1,2\\
\gcd(d^{-1}Q_{1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),d^{-1}Q_{2}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),P(z))=1\end{subarray}}\hskip-14.22636pt1\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{prim}}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|\sim Y/k\\
d\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}\hskip-14.22636pt1.

Breaking the
d
-sum into dyadic intervals, it follows from
Corollary
5.14
and (
6.5
) that

R_{k}\ll_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{Y}{k}\right)^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{(Y/k)^{4}\log Y}{D_{1}}\ll\frac{Y^{2+\varepsilon}}{k^{2}}+\frac{B\log B}{k^{4}},

for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Hence

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k>1\\
\gcd(k,P(z))=1\end{subarray}}R_{k}\ll_{\varepsilon}Y^{2+\varepsilon}+B\log B\sum_{k>z}\frac{1}{k^{4}}.

The remaining sum is
O(z^{-3})
, which thereby completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We now turn our attention to an asymptotic evaluation of the main term
\Sigma_{1}
in the previous lemma.
We use the geometry of numbers to prove the following result.

Lemma 6.4
.

We have

\Sigma_{1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{3}\ll D_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})}{\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid\frac{P(z)}{\ell_{1}}\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\\
\gcd(e,\ell_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.5359pt\frac{\mu(e)}{e^{3}}S_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3},e}+O_{\eta}\left(\left(2^{z}+P(z)^{4}\right)B^{1-\frac{\eta}{2}}\right),

where

S_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3},e}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\\
\gcd(d,\ell_{1})=1\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}\frac{\#V_{de}^{\times}\cdot\Vol\mathscr{B}(Y,d)}{d^{3}}.

Proof.

Using Möbius inversion to handle

\gcd(d^{-1}Q_{1}(\mathbf{y}),d^{-1}Q_{2}(\mathbf{y}),P(z))=1
, we see that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\mid P(z)}\mu(e)\sum_{D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
\gcd(\mathbf{y},dP(z))=1\\
de\mid Q_{i}(\mathbf{y}),~i=1,2\end{subarray}}1=\sum_{e\mid P(z)}\mu(e)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
\gcd(\mathbf{y},dP(z))=1\\
\mathbf{y}\in\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],de}\end{subarray}}1,

where
V_{de}^{\times}
and
\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],de}
are given by
(
3.8
) and (
3.9
), respectively.
Appealing to Möbius inversion once more, we obtain

\Sigma_{1}=\sum_{e\mid P(z)}\mu(e)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
\mathbf{y}\in\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],de}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\mid\mathbf{y}\\
\ell\mid dP(z)\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell).

It will be convenient to observe that

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\mid\mathbf{y}\\
\ell\mid dP(z)\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}c\mid\mathbf{y}\\
c\mid de\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid\mathbf{y}\\
\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\gcd(\ell_{1},de)=1\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell_{1})\mu(c)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid\mathbf{y}\\
\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\gcd(\ell_{1},de)=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{2}\mid\mathbf{y}\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{3}\mid\mathbf{y}\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\\
\gcd(\ell_{3},e)=1\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell_{1})\mu(\ell_{2})\mu(\ell_{3}).\end{split}

Note that
\mu(\ell_{1})\mu(\ell_{2})\mu(\ell_{3})=\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})
in the summand.
But then it follows that

\begin{split}\Sigma_{1}&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{3}\ll D_{2}\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid\frac{P(z)}{\ell_{1}}\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\\
\gcd(e,\ell_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\mu(e)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\\
\gcd(d,\ell_{1})=1\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}\sum_{[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\cap\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\\
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\mid\mathbf{y}\\
\mathbf{y}\in\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],de}\end{subarray}}1\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{3}\ll D_{2}\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid\frac{P(z)}{\ell_{1}}\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\\
\gcd(e,\ell_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\mu(e)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\\
\gcd(d,\ell_{1})=1\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}\sum_{[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}}N,\end{split}

where

N=\#\left(\mathbb{Z}^{4}\cap(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})^{-1}\mathscr{B}(Y,d)\cap\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}}\right).

Recalling the definition (
6.4
) of
\mathscr{B}(Y,d)
,
we now appeal to the lattice point counting result worked out by Schmidt
[
23
, Lemma
2
]
. This yields

N=\frac{\Vol\left((\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})^{-1}\mathscr{B}\left(Y,d\right)\right)\cdot(\ell_{2}\ell_{3})^{3}}{(de)^{3}}+O\left(1+\frac{Y^{3}}{(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})^{3}s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}}^{3}}\right),

where
s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}}
is the smallest successive minimum of the lattice

\Lambda_{[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}}
.

Since

\Vol\left((\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})^{-1}\mathscr{B}\left(Y,d\right)\right)=(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})^{-4}\Vol\mathscr{B}(Y,d),

it follows that

(6.8)

N=\frac{\Vol\mathscr{B}(Y,d)}{(de)^{3}\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}+O\left(1+\frac{Y^{3}}{(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})^{3}s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}}^{3}}\right).

We begin by handling the overall contribution to
\Sigma_{1}
from the error terms.
Let
E_{1}
denote the overall contribution from the term
O(1)
and let
E_{2}
denote the contribution from the term involving the first successive minimum. Beginning with the latter,
it follows from
Lemma
5.12
that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}\sum_{[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}}\frac{Y^{3}}{(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})^{3}s_{1,[\mathbf{u}],\frac{de}{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}}^{3}}\ll\frac{Y^{3}}{\ell_{1}^{3}(\ell_{2}\ell_{3})^{\frac{7}{4}}}\cdot(\ell_{2,\Delta}\ell_{3,\Delta})\cdot(D_{1}e)^{-\frac{1}{4}}\frac{\log B}{\log\log B},

making the overall contribution

E_{2}\ll B^{1-\eta}\frac{\log B}{\log\log B}\sum_{\ell_{2}\mid P(z)}\frac{\ell_{2,\Delta}}{\ell_{2}^{\frac{7}{4}}}\sum_{\ell_{3}\ll D_{2}}\frac{\ell_{3,\Delta}}{\ell_{3}^{\frac{7}{4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\end{subarray}}e^{-\frac{1}{4}},

since
D_{1}
satisfies (
6.5
) and
Y\ll B^{\frac{3}{8}-\frac{\eta}{2}}
.
The inner sum is at most
2^{z}
and the sums over
\ell_{2},\ell_{3}
are

O(1)
, since

\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\ell_{\Delta}}{\ell^{\frac{7}{4}}}\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}\\
p\mid k\Rightarrow p\mid\Delta\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{k^{\frac{3}{4}}}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{{\ell^{\prime}}^{\frac{7}{4}}}\ll 1.

Hence

E_{2}=O_{\eta}(2^{z}B^{1-\frac{\eta}{2}})
, which is satisfactory.

The remaining error term in the lattice point counting result makes the contribution

(6.9)

E_{1}\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{3}\ll D_{2}\end{subarray}}|\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}\#V_{de}^{\times}

to
\Sigma_{1}
.
We claim that

(6.10)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\ll D_{2}\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}\#V_{de}^{\times}\ll P(z)^{2}D_{2}^{2}\frac{(\ell_{2,\Delta}\ell_{3,\Delta})^{\varepsilon}}{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\\
p\nmid\Delta\end{subarray}}\left(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})\right).

To prove this, we appeal to the second part of Lemma
3.5
, which implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\leqslant D\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}\#V_{de}^{\times}

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\leqslant D\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}ded_{\Delta}^{\varepsilon}e_{\Delta}^{\varepsilon}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid de\\
p\nmid\Delta\end{subarray}}\left(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})\right)

\displaystyle\ll ee_{\Delta}^{\varepsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\leqslant D/\ell_{3}\end{subarray}}\ell_{3}d^{\prime}(\ell_{3,\Delta}d_{\Delta}^{\prime})^{\varepsilon}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid\ell_{3}d^{\prime}e\\
p\nmid\Delta\end{subarray}}\left(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})\right),

for any
D\geqslant 1
,
on writing
d=\ell_{3}d^{\prime}
.
Now

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\leqslant D/\ell_{3}\end{subarray}}d^{\prime}d_{\Delta}^{\prime\varepsilon}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid d^{\prime}\\
p\nmid\Delta\end{subarray}}\left(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})\right)

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}\\
p\mid k\Rightarrow p\mid\Delta\end{subarray}}k^{1+\varepsilon}\sum_{d^{\prime\prime}\leqslant D/(k\ell_{3})}d^{\prime\prime}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid d^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}\left(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})\right)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{D^{2}}{\ell_{3}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}\\
p\mid k\Rightarrow p\mid\Delta\end{subarray}}k^{-1+\varepsilon}.

This is
O_{\varepsilon}({D^{2}}/{\ell_{3}^{2}})
.
Hence we have proved that

(6.11)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\leqslant D\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}\#V_{de}^{\times}\ll\frac{ee_{\Delta}^{\varepsilon}\ell_{3,\Delta}^{\varepsilon}D^{2}}{\ell_{3}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid\ell_{3}e\\
p\nmid\Delta\end{subarray}}\left(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})\right),

for any
D\geqslant 1
.
Making the change
of variable
e=\ell_{2}e^{\prime}
,
and noting that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e^{\prime}\mid\frac{P(z)}{\ell_{2}}\end{subarray}}e^{\prime}e_{\Delta}^{\prime\varepsilon}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid e^{\prime}\\
p\nmid\Delta\end{subarray}}\left(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})\right)\ll\frac{P(z)^{2}}{\ell_{2}^{2}},

the claimed bound (
6.10
) readily follow.

It
follows from (
6.5
) and

Y\ll B^{\frac{3}{8}-\frac{\eta}{2}}
that

D_{2}^{2}\ll B^{1-4\eta}
.
On inserting (
6.10
) into (
6.9
) and summing trivially over
\ell_{1}
, a similar analysis leads to the conclusion that

E_{1}\ll_{\varepsilon}P(z)^{3}D_{2}^{2}\sum_{\ell_{2}\mid P(z)}\sum_{\ell_{3}\ll D_{2}}\frac{(\ell_{2,\Delta}\ell_{3,\Delta})^{\varepsilon}}{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\\
p\nmid\Delta\end{subarray}}\left(1+O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})\right)\\
\ll_{\eta}P(z)^{4}B^{1-\frac{\eta}{2}},

which is satisfactory for the lemma.

Finally, we note that the main term in our asymptotic formula (
6.8
) for
N
gives

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{3}\ll D_{2}\end{subarray}}\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid\frac{P(z)}{\ell_{1}}\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\\
\gcd(e,\ell_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\mu(e)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\\
\gcd(d,\ell_{1})=1\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}\sum_{[\mathbf{u}]\in V_{de}^{\times}}\frac{\Vol\mathscr{B}(Y,d)}{(de)^{3}\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}},

once inserted into our expression for
\Sigma_{1}
.
Finally, on rearranging the terms, we are led to the main term in the lemma.
∎

We now have everything in place to complete the first step in the
proof of Proposition
6.1
. We shall take

z=\frac{\log B}{(\log\log B)^{2}}\quad\text{ and }\quad M=B^{\frac{\eta}{10}}.

Mertens’ theorem implies that
P(z)\ll\operatorname{e}^{\frac{\log B}{(\log\log B)^{2}}}\ll_{\eta}B^{\frac{\eta}{16}}
.
Hence we obtain

\Sigma_{1}=\hskip-2.84544pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{3}\ll D\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})}{\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid\frac{P(z)}{\ell_{1}}\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\\
\gcd(e,\ell_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.5359pt\frac{\mu(e)}{e^{3}}S_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3},e}+O_{\eta}\left(B^{1-\frac{\eta}{4}}\right),

in Lemma
6.4
.
Next, it follows from Lemma
6.2
that

\displaystyle T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{B}{z^{1-\varepsilon}}+B^{1-\frac{\eta}{2}}M^{3}+Y^{2+\varepsilon}+\frac{B(\log B)^{2}}{M}\ll\frac{B}{\sqrt{\log B}}.

since
Y\ll B^{\frac{3}{8}-\frac{\eta}{2}}
.
Inserting these estimates into (
6.7
), we obtain

\#\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(Y)=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{3}\ll D_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})}{\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid\frac{P(z)}{\ell_{1}}\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\\
\gcd(e,\ell_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(e)}{e^{3}}S_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3},e}+\left(\frac{B}{\sqrt{\log B}}\right).

We next show that the sum over
\ell_{3}
can be truncated with acceptable error,
as in the following result.

Lemma 6.5
.

We have

\#\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(Y)=2T_{0}+\left(\frac{B}{\sqrt{\log B}}\right),

where

T_{0}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{3}\mid P(z)\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})}{\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid\frac{P(z)}{\ell_{1}}\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\\
\gcd(e,\ell_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.5359pt\frac{\mu(e)}{e^{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\\
\gcd(d,\ell_{1})=1\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}\frac{\#V_{de}^{\times}\cdot\Vol\mathscr{B}(Y,d)}{d^{3}}.

Proof.

Recall the definition of
S_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3},e}
from the statement of Lemma
6.4
.
Then, in order to prove the lemma, it will suffice to bound

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{3}\ll D_{2}\\
\ell_{3}\nmid P(z)\end{subarray}}\frac{|\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})|}{\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\end{subarray}}\frac{|\mu(e)|}{e^{3}}S_{\ell_{1},\ell_{3},e}

\displaystyle\ll Y^{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{3}\ll D_{2}\\
\ell_{3}\nmid P(z)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid e\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{e^{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}\ll d\ll D_{2}\\
\ell_{3}\mid d\end{subarray}}\frac{\#V_{de}^{\times}}{d^{3}}.

A modest reworking of the proof of
(
6.10
), using partial summation and (
6.11
) to incorporate the weight
(de)^{-3}
, easily yields

E\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{Y^{4}}{D_{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{3}\ll D_{2}\\
\ell_{3}\nmid P(z)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}^{3-\varepsilon}\ell_{3}^{2-\varepsilon}}\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{Y^{4}}{D_{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{3}>z\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\ell_{3}^{2-\varepsilon}}\ll\frac{B}{z^{1-\varepsilon}},

by (
6.5
).
Our choice of
z
ensures this is satisfactory.
∎

6.3.
Asymptotic formula for
T_{0}

The final step in the proof of Proposition
6.1

is to analyse the main term in
Lemma
6.5
, in which we recall that

\mathscr{B}(Y,d)
is given by (
6.4
).
Making the change of variables
e=\ell_{2}e^{\prime}
and
d=\ell_{3}d^{\prime}
, we find that

(6.12)

T_{0}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{3}\mid P(z)\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})\mu(\ell_{2})}{\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}^{4}\ell_{3}^{4}}\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e^{\prime}\mid\frac{P(z)}{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\\
\gcd(e^{\prime},\ell_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.5359pt\frac{\mu(e^{\prime})}{e^{\prime 3}}Q_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},e^{\prime}},

where

(6.13)

\begin{split}Q_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},e^{\prime}}&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1}/\ell_{3}\ll d^{\prime}\ll D_{2}/\ell_{3}\\
\gcd(d^{\prime},\ell_{1})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\#V_{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}d^{\prime}e^{\prime}}^{\times}\cdot\Vol\mathscr{B}(Y,\ell_{3}d^{\prime})}{d^{\prime 3}}\\
&=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{1,\mathbf{y}}\leqslant d^{\prime}\leqslant D_{2,\mathbf{y}}\\
\gcd(d^{\prime},\ell_{1})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\#V_{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}d^{\prime}e^{\prime}}^{\times}}{d^{\prime 3}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\end{split}

and

(6.14)

D_{1,\mathbf{y}}=\frac{|\mathbf{y}|^{2}\max(|Q_{1}(\mathbf{y})|,|Q_{2}(\mathbf{y})|)}{\ell_{3}B},\quad D_{2,\mathbf{y}}=\frac{\max(|Q_{1}(\mathbf{y})|,|Q_{2}(\mathbf{y})|)}{\ell_{3}B^{\frac{1}{4}+\eta}}.

Here, we have observed that the condition
D_{1}/\ell_{1}\ll d^{\prime}\ll D_{2}/\ell_{3}
is implied by the condition

D_{1,\mathbf{y}}\leqslant d^{\prime}\leqslant D_{2,\mathbf{y}}
, since
Z(\mathbb{R})=\emptyset
.
We are therefore led to prove the following result.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
Z_{2}>Z_{1}>0
and let
c,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
be square-free coprime integers. Then

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z_{1}\leqslant d\leqslant Z_{2}\\
(d,\ell)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\#V_{cd}^{\times}}{d^{3}}&=C\#V_{c}^{\times}h_{1}(c)h_{2}(\ell)\left(\frac{1}{Z_{1}}-\frac{1}{Z_{2}}\right)+O_{\varepsilon}(c^{1+\varepsilon}Z_{1}^{-\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon}),\end{split}

for any
\varepsilon>0
, where

\begin{split}C&=\prod_{p}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{\#V_{p^{a}}^{\times}}{p^{2a}}\right)\\
h_{1}(c)&=\prod_{p|c}\left(1+\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{\#V_{p^{a}}^{\times}}{p^{2a}}\right)^{-1}\left(1+\frac{1}{\#V_{p}^{\times}}\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{\#V_{p^{a+1}}^{\times}}{p^{2a}}\right)\\
h_{2}(\ell)&=\prod_{p|\ell}\left(1+\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{\#V_{p^{a}}^{\times}}{p^{2a}}\right)^{-1}.\end{split}

Moreover,
h_{1}(c)=O_{\varepsilon}(c^{\varepsilon})
and
h_{2}(\ell)=O_{\varepsilon}(\ell^{\varepsilon})
, for any
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

If
0<Z_{1}<1
, the result is trivial, so we may assume
Z_{1}\geqslant 1
. We begin by defining the multiplicative function

f(p^{a})=\begin{cases}p^{-a}\#V_{p^{a}}^{\times}&\text{ if $p\nmid c\ell$,}\\
p^{-a}\#V_{p^{a+1}}^{\times}/\#V_{p}^{\times}&\text{ if $p\mid c$,}\\
0&\text{ if $p\mid\ell$.}\end{cases}

It follows form the Hasse bound that

\#V_{p}^{\times}=p-T_{p}+1,

where

T_{p}=O(\sqrt{p})
.
Moreover, the Chinese remainder theorem implies that

f(d)=\frac{\#V_{cd}^{\times}}{d\#V_{c}^{\times}}

for any

d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\gcd(d,\ell)=1
.
Hence we can write

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z_{1}\leqslant d\leqslant Z_{2}\\
(d,\ell)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\#V_{cd}^{\times}}{d^{3}}=\#V_{c}^{\times}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z_{1}\leqslant d\leqslant Z_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{f(d)}{d^{2}}.

We claim that

(6.15)

\sum_{d\leqslant x}f(d)=Ch_{1}(c)h_{2}(\ell)x+O\left(x^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}\right),

for any
\varepsilon>0
,
where
C,h_{1},h_{2}
are defined in the statement of the lemma.
Once achieved, on recalling the bound
\#V_{c}^{\times}=O_{\varepsilon}(c^{1+\varepsilon})
from Lemma
3.5
,
an application of partial summation easily leads to the statement of the lemma.
Finally, the bounds on
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
in the last part of the lemma follow easily from
Lemma
3.5
.

To prove (
6.15
), we write
f=1*g
as a Dirichlet convolution, noting that

g(p^{a})=f(p^{a})-f(p^{a-1})

for any prime
p
and
a\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose first that
p\nmid\Delta
. Then it follows from Corollary
3.6
that

g(p^{a})=\begin{cases}O(p^{-\frac{1}{2}})&\text{ if $a=1$ and $p\nmid\ell$,}\\
-1&\text{ if $a=1$ and $p\mid\ell$,}\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

On the other hand, if
p\mid\Delta
then (
3.13
)
and Lemma
3.4
together yield

g(p^{a})=O(a).

Given any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
it therefore follows that

g(k)\ll_{\varepsilon}k^{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{k_{1}},

where
k_{1}
is the part of
k
that is coprime to
\ell\Delta
.
Given this, we easily conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{d\leqslant x}f(d)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\leqslant x}g(k)\left(\frac{x}{k}+O(1)\right)=\gamma x+O(x^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}),

for any
\varepsilon>0
, where

\gamma=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{g(k)}{k}=\prod_{p}\left(1+\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{g(p^{a})}{p^{a}}\right)=\prod_{p}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{f(p^{a})}{p^{a}}\right).

Inserting the definition of
f(p^{a})
, it is straightforward to see that

\gamma=Ch_{1}(c)h_{2}(\ell)
, as required to complete the proof of the lemma.
∎

We now seek to
apply this in (
6.13
).
Note from (
6.14
)
that
D_{1,\mathbf{y}}\gg Y^{4}/(\ell_{3}B)
, since we are assuming that
Z(\mathbb{R})=\emptyset
. Hence
we obtain

\displaystyle Q_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},e^{\prime}}=~

\displaystyle C\#V_{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}e^{\prime}}^{\times}h_{1}(\ell_{2}\ell_{3}e^{\prime})h_{2}(\ell_{1})\nu(Y)+O_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{(\ell_{2}e^{\prime})^{1+\varepsilon}\ell_{3}^{\frac{5}{2}}B^{\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon}}{Y^{2}}\right),

where

\nu(Y)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\\
|\mathbf{y}|\sim Y\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{1}{D_{1,\mathbf{y}}}-\frac{1}{D_{2,\mathbf{y}}}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

and
D_{1,\mathbf{y}},D_{2,\mathbf{y}}
are given by (
6.14
).
Clearly

\nu(Y)=\ell_{3}B\sigma_{\infty}(Y)+O(\ell_{3}B^{\frac{1}{4}+\eta}Y^{2}),

in the notation of (
6.3
).
Thus
Q_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},e^{\prime}}
is equal to

\displaystyle C\#V_{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}e^{\prime}}^{\times}h_{1}(\ell_{2}\ell_{3}e^{\prime})h_{2}(\ell_{1})\ell_{3}B\left(\sigma_{\infty}(Y)+O\left(\frac{Y^{2}}{B^{\frac{3}{4}-\eta}}\right)\right)+O_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{(\ell_{2}e^{\prime})^{1+\varepsilon}\ell_{3}^{\frac{5}{2}}B^{\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon}}{Y^{2}}\right).

It is now time to insert this estimate into
(
6.12
). First, the overall contribution from the error term is

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{B^{\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon}}{Y^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{3}\mid P(z)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}^{3-\varepsilon}\ell_{3}^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e^{\prime}\mid\frac{P(z)}{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{e^{\prime 2-\varepsilon}}\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{B^{\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon}}{Y^{2}}.

Adjoining the contribution from the main term, we therefore obtain

(6.16)

T_{0}=C\cdot J(z)\cdot B\left(\sigma_{\infty}(Y)+O\left(\frac{Y^{2}}{B^{\frac{3}{4}-\eta}}\right)\right)+O_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{B^{\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon}}{Y^{2}}\right),

for any
\varepsilon>0
,
where

\displaystyle J(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{2}\mid P(z)\\
\ell_{3}\mid P(z)\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3})\mu(\ell_{2})h_{1}(\ell_{2}\ell_{3}e^{\prime})h_{2}(\ell_{1})}{\ell_{1}^{4}\ell_{2}^{4}\ell_{3}^{3}}\hskip-8.5359pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e^{\prime}\mid\frac{P(z)}{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\\
\gcd(e^{\prime},\ell_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.5359pt\frac{\mu(e^{\prime})}{e^{\prime 3}}\cdot\#V_{\ell_{2}\ell_{3}e^{\prime}}^{\times}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\mid P(z)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{4}\in\mathbb{N}\\
\ell_{1}\cdots\ell_{4}=m\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.69046pt\frac{\mu(\ell_{1})h_{2}(\ell_{1})}{\ell_{1}^{4}}\cdot\frac{\mu(\ell_{2})^{2}h_{1}(\ell_{2})\#V_{\ell_{2}}^{\times}}{\ell_{2}^{4}}\frac{\mu(\ell_{3})h_{1}(\ell_{3})\#V_{\ell_{3}}^{\times}}{\ell_{3}^{3}}\cdot\frac{\mu(\ell_{4})h_{1}(\ell_{4})\#V_{\ell_{4}}^{\times}}{\ell_{4}^{3}}.

In view of the second part of Lemma
3.5
and the bounds on
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
from Lemma

6.6
,
we can extend the sum over
m
to all square-free integers, finding that

J(z)=J+O(z^{-1}),

where

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\prod_{p}\left(1-\frac{h_{2}(p)}{p^{4}}+\frac{h_{1}(p)\#V_{p}^{\times}}{p^{4}}-\frac{2h_{1}(p)\#V_{p}^{\times}}{p^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{p}\left(1+\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{\#V_{p^{a}}^{\times}}{p^{2a}}\right)^{-1}\left(1+\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{\#V_{p^{a}}^{\times}}{p^{2a}}-\frac{1}{p^{4}}+\left(\frac{1}{p^{2}}-\frac{2}{p}\right)\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{\#V_{p^{a}}^{\times}}{p^{2a}}\right).

Recalling the definition of
C
from the statement of Lemma
6.6
, it follows that

CJ=\mathfrak{S}_{1}
, in the notation of (
2.8
).
Returning to (
6.16
) and observing that
z\geqslant\sqrt{\log B}
, it therefore follows that

T_{0}=\mathfrak{S}_{1}B\left(\sigma_{\infty}(Y)+O\left(\frac{Y^{2}}{B^{\frac{3}{4}-\eta}}\right)\right)+O\left(\frac{B}{\sqrt{\log B}}\right)+O_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{B^{\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon}}{Y^{2}}\right),

where
\sigma_{\infty}(Y)
is given by (
6.3
).
Substituting this into
Lemma
6.5
,
and using the lower bound
Y\gg B^{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\eta}{2}}
,
we are finally led to the statement of Proposition
6.1
.

7.
Asymptotics via the circle method

The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
2.3
.
Recall the notation

\varDelta(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{4}L_{i}(\mathbf{x})

from (
5.16
).
For any
\mathbf{x}
and any compactly supported weight function
w:\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
,
the singular integral is defined to be

\sigma_{\infty,w}(\mathbf{x})=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}w(\mathbf{y})\operatorname{e}\left(-\theta(L_{1}(\mathbf{x})y_{1}^{2}+\cdots+L_{4}(\mathbf{x})y_{4}^{2})\right)\operatorname{d}\mathbf{y}\operatorname{d}\theta.

In the special case that
w_{0}
is the characteristic function of
[-1,1]^{4}
, we set

\sigma_{\infty}(\mathbf{x})=\sigma_{\infty,w_{0}}(\mathbf{x}).

We have
\sigma_{\infty}(\lambda\mathbf{x})=\lambda^{-1}\sigma_{\infty}(\mathbf{x})
, for any
\lambda>0
.
Moreover, it follows from
[
6
, Lemma 4.12]
that

(7.1)

\sigma_{\infty}(\mathbf{x})\ll\frac{1}{|\varDelta(\mathbf{x})|^{1/4}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\sigma_{\infty,w}(\mathbf{x})\ll\frac{1}{|\varDelta(\mathbf{x})|^{1/4}},

for any
compactly supported smooth weight function
w:\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
.

Finally, we put

\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{S}(Q_{\mathbf{x}})=\prod_{p}\sigma_{p}(\mathbf{x}),

where

\sigma_{p}(\mathbf{x})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\#\left\{\mathbf{y}\in(\mathbb{Z}/p^{k}\mathbb{Z})^{4}:L_{1}(\mathbf{x})y_{1}^{2}+\cdots+L_{4}(\mathbf{x})y_{4}^{2}\equiv 0\bmod{p^{k}}\right\}}{p^{3k}}.

With this notation to hand we may now record the first main result of this section, which closely follows the strategy in
[
6
, § 5]
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
\eta>0
. Then

\displaystyle\#{\mathscr{L}}_{2}(B)=~

\displaystyle\frac{B}{\zeta(2)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
B^{2\eta}\leqslant|\mathbf{x}|\leqslant B^{\frac{1}{4}}\\
\prod_{i=1}^{4}L_{i}(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\infty}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|}+O(\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}B\log B)+O_{\eta}(B^{1-\frac{\eta^{2}}{32}}).

The asymptotic behaviour of the leading term in Proposition
7.1
is our next milestone and
is summarised in the following result.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
\eta>0
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
B^{2\eta}\leqslant|\mathbf{x}|\leqslant B^{\frac{1}{4}}\\
\prod_{i=1}^{4}L_{i}(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\infty}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|}=\frac{\tau_{\infty}\mathfrak{S}_{2}}{4\zeta(2)}\log B+O(\eta\log B)+O_{\eta}(1),

where
\tau_{\infty}
is given by (
2.9
) and
\mathfrak{S}_{2}
is given by (
2.11
).

Combining Propositions
7.1
and
7.2
concludes the proof of Proposition
2.3
.

7.1.
Proof of Proposition
7.1

Let

w:\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
be a compactly supported weight function.
Then for any
X\geqslant 1
, we define the weighted counting function

(7.2)

L_{w}(B,X)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim X\\
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{4}\\
\eqref{eq:quadbundle}\text{ holds}\end{subarray}}w\left(\frac{|\mathbf{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{B^{\frac{1}{2}}}\mathbf{y}\right).

When
w=w_{0}
, as above, then

\#{\mathscr{L}}_{2}(B)=\sum_{X\nearrow B^{\frac{1}{4}}}L_{w_{0}}(B,X).

Our strategy for proving Proposition
7.1
is to first produce an asymptotic formula for

L_{w}(B,X)
when
w
is a suitable smooth weight, before finally showing how to approximate the counting function

L_{w_{0}}(B,X)
by smoothly weighted ones.
It turns out that the circle method tools required to produce an asymptotic formula for

L_{w}(B,X)
are already in exactly the right form in
[
6
]
.
This allows us to prove the following analogue of

[
6
, Lemma 5.3]
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let

w:\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
be a compactly supported weight function
which vanishes on
[-\eta,\eta]^{4}
.
Suppose that
X\geqslant 1
satisfies

(7.3)

B^{2\eta}\ll X\ll B^{\frac{1}{4}-4\eta}.

Then, for
\eta>0
sufficiently small, we have

L_{w}(B,X)=\frac{B}{\zeta(2)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim X\\
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\infty,w}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|}+O_{\eta,w}(B^{1-\frac{1}{16}\eta^{2}}).

Proof.

For
\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}
and

Y\gg 1
, we define

N_{w}(Q_{\mathbf{x}};Y)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{4}\\
Q_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y})=0\end{subarray}}w\left(\frac{\mathbf{y}}{Y}\right),

where
Q_{\mathbf{x}}
is given by (
5.12
). Then

L_{w}(B,X)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim X\\
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\end{subarray}}N_{w}\left(Q_{\mathbf{x}};\sqrt{\frac{B}{|\mathbf{x}|}}\right).

As in (
5.16
), we write
\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|=\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 4}|L_{i}(\mathbf{x})|
and we recall the definition (
5.1
) of
\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})
.

It now follows from
[
6
, Lemma 5.2]

N_{w}(Q_{\mathbf{x}};Y)=\frac{\sigma_{\infty,w}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{\zeta(2)}Y^{2}+O_{\eta,w}\left(\frac{Y^{\frac{5}{3}+5\eta}}{\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right),

for any
Y\geqslant 1
, provided that

Y^{\eta}\leqslant\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|\leqslant Y^{\frac{2}{3}}
,

|L_{i}(\mathbf{x})|\geqslant\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|^{1-\eta}
for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant 4
, and

\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})\leqslant\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|^{\eta}
.
Observing that
|\mathbf{x}|\ll\|Q_{\mathbf{x}}\|\ll|\mathbf{x}|
, we deduce that

N_{w}(Q_{\mathbf{x}};Y)=\frac{\sigma_{\infty,w}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{\zeta(2)}Y^{2}+O_{\eta,w}\left(\frac{Y^{\frac{5}{3}+5\eta}}{|\mathbf{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right),

provided that

(7.4)

Y^{\eta}\ll|\mathbf{x}|\ll Y^{\frac{2}{3}},

(7.5)

|L_{i}(\mathbf{x})|\gg|\mathbf{x}|^{1-\eta},\text{ for every }1\leqslant i\leqslant 4,

and

(7.6)

\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})\ll|\mathbf{x}|^{\eta}.

Under the assumption that
X
satisfies (
7.3
), the condition (
7.4
) is always satisfied with
Y=\sqrt{B/|\mathbf{x}|}
. Hence it follows that

(7.7)

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2},~|\mathbf{x}|\sim X\\
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\\
\eqref{eq:cond2}\eqref{eq:cond3}\text{ hold}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{4}\\
\eqref{eq:quadbundle}\text{ holds}\end{subarray}}w\left(\frac{|\mathbf{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{B^{\frac{1}{2}}}\mathbf{y}\right)=~&\frac{B}{\zeta(2)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2},|\mathbf{x}|\sim X\\
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\\
\eqref{eq:cond2}\eqref{eq:cond3}\text{ hold}\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\infty,w}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|}+O_{\eta,w}(B^{\frac{5}{6}+\frac{5}{2}\eta}X^{\frac{2}{3}}).\end{split}

Note that the error term is
O(B^{1-\frac{1}{6}\eta})
, since
X\ll B^{\frac{1}{4}-4\eta}
in (
7.3
).

It remains to treat the cases where either (
7.5
) or (
7.6
) fails.
We start with (
7.5
) and assume without loss of generality that
|L_{1}(\mathbf{x})|\ll|\mathbf{x}|^{1-\eta}
.
Then it follows from Lemma
5.8
, with
\delta=1-\eta
and
Y=\sqrt{B/X}
, that the overall contribution is

\displaystyle\ll_{\eta,\varepsilon}B^{\varepsilon}X^{-\frac{1}{2}\eta}\left(B+B^{\frac{1}{2}}X^{2}\right)\ll X^{-\frac{1}{2}\eta}B^{1+\varepsilon}\ll B^{1-\eta^{2}+\varepsilon},

under the assumption (
7.3
).
On taking
\varepsilon=\eta^{2}/2
, the contribution from this case is therefore
O_{\eta}(B^{1-\eta^{2}/2})
.
To handle the situation when (
7.6
) fails, we use Proposition
5.3
. Taking
D\gg X^{\eta}
, we therefore obtain the contribution

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}B^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{B+B^{\frac{1}{2}}X^{2}}{X^{\frac{\eta}{16}}}+XB^{\frac{1}{2}}+B^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)\ll X^{-\frac{\eta}{16}}B^{1+\varepsilon}+B^{\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon}\ll B^{1-\frac{\eta^{2}}{8}+\varepsilon}.

Taking
\varepsilon=\eta^{2}/{16}
, we obtain the overall contribution
O_{\eta}(B^{1-\eta^{2}/16})
.

It remains to extend the sum of
\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}
in (
7.7
) to the whole range.
For this purpose, we consider the sums

(7.8)

S_{1}(X)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2},~\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim X,~|L_{1}(\mathbf{x})|\ll X^{1-\eta}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}||\prod_{i=1}^{4}L_{i}(\mathbf{x})|^{\frac{1}{4}}}

and

(7.9)

S_{2}(X)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2},~\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim X,~\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})\gg X^{\eta}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}||\prod_{i=1}^{4}L_{i}(\mathbf{x})|^{\frac{1}{4}}}.

In both of these sums
we can apply Lemma
4.3
to estimate
\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})
.

We start by estimating (
7.8
). Combining Lemmas
4.3
and
5.7

with (
5.27
), we obtain

\displaystyle S_{1}(X)

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon,\eta}\frac{1}{X^{\frac{7}{4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2},~\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim X,~|L_{1}(\mathbf{x})|\ll X^{1-\eta}\end{subarray}}\frac{\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})^{\varepsilon}L(1,\chi_{Q_{\mathbf{x}}})}{|L_{1}(\mathbf{x})|^{\frac{1}{4}}}

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon,\eta}\frac{X^{5\varepsilon}}{X^{\frac{7}{4}}}\sum_{R\nearrow X^{1-\eta}}\frac{1}{R^{\frac{1}{4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2},~\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim X,~|L_{1}(\mathbf{x})|\sim R\end{subarray}}1,

on introducing a dyadic parameter for the range of
|L_{1}(\mathbf{x})|
.
It follows easily that

S_{1}(X)\ll_{\varepsilon,\eta}\frac{X^{5\varepsilon}}{X^{\frac{7}{4}}}\sum_{R\nearrow X^{1-\eta}}XR^{\frac{3}{4}}\ll X^{-\frac{3}{4}\eta+5\varepsilon}\ll X^{-\frac{\eta}{2}},

by fixing
\varepsilon
to be sufficiently small.

Now we handle
S_{2}(X)
similarly. It follows from Lemma
4.3
and (
5.27
) that

\displaystyle S_{2}(X)

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{X^{5\varepsilon}}{X^{\frac{7}{4}}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{4}\sum_{R\nearrow X}\frac{1}{R^{\frac{1}{4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2},~\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim X,~|L_{i_{1}}(\mathbf{x})|\sim R\\
\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})\gg X^{\eta}\end{subarray}}1.

The condition
\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})\gg X^{\eta}
implies in particular
\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})\gg(XR)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}
. Applying Lemma
5.9
with

\delta=\frac{\eta}{2}
and
S=X
, we obtain

S_{2}(X)\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{X^{5\varepsilon}}{X^{\frac{7}{4}}}\sum_{R\nearrow X}\frac{XR^{1-\frac{\eta}{16}}}{R^{\frac{1}{4}}}\ll X^{-\frac{\eta}{16}+5\varepsilon}.

Thus

S_{2}(X)\ll X^{-\frac{\eta}{32}},

on taking
\varepsilon
sufficiently small.

Invoking the bound (
7.1
) for
\sigma_{\infty,w}(\mathbf{x})
, we may now apply
our bounds for
S_{1}(X)
and
S_{2}(X)
to deduce that
there is a satisfactory overall contribution to the main term in (
7.7
), corresponding to the failure of (
7.5
) or (
7.6
).
The proof of the lemma is now completed.
∎

It remains to remove the smooth weights, using the previous result to deduce a similar asymptotic formula for the counting function
L_{w_{0}}(B,X)
, where
w_{0}
is the characteristic function of
[-1,1]^{4}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Assume that
X
lies in the range (
7.3
).
Then, for
\eta>0
sufficiently small, we have

L_{w_{0}}(B,X)=\frac{B}{\zeta(2)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim X\\
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\infty}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|}+O(\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}B)+O_{\eta}(B^{1-\frac{1}{16}\eta^{2}}).

Proof.

We mimic the procedure of
[
6
, §5.3]
, which relates the counting function

L_{w_{0}}(B,X)
to one in which smooth weights appear. For each
\eta>0
sufficiently small, we fix two smooth weight functions
w_{1},w_{2}
satisfying the requirements of
[
6
, Lemma 4.13]
. Thus

(7.10)

\sigma_{\infty,w_{i}}(\mathbf{x})-\sigma_{\infty}(\mathbf{x})\ll\frac{\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\prod_{i=1}^{4}|L_{i}(\mathbf{x})|^{\frac{1}{4}}}.

Moreover,

L_{w_{1}}(B,X)\leqslant L_{w_{0}}(B,X)\leqslant L_{w_{0}}(4\eta^{2}B,X)+L_{w_{2}}(B,X).

We can apply Lemma

7.3
to estimate
L_{w_{1}}(B,X)
and
L_{w_{2}}(B,X)
.
Moreover, on recalling (
7.2
), we deduce from Theorem
2.1
that

\displaystyle L_{w_{0}}(4\eta^{2}B,X)

\displaystyle\ll\eta^{2}B+\eta^{4/3}X^{4/3}B^{2/3}\ll\eta B,

since
X\ll B^{\frac{1}{4}}
in
(
7.3
).

In view of (
7.10
), for
i=1,2
,
it remains to show that

(7.11)

S_{0}(X)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim X\\
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}||\prod_{i=1}^{4}L_{i}(\mathbf{x})|^{\frac{1}{4}}}=O(1),

in order to complete the proof of the lemma. We can adopt a similar argument to the treatment of
S_{1}(X)
and
S_{2}(X)
in (
7.8
) and (
7.9
), respectively.
Appealing to Lemma
4.3
and breaking into dyadic intervals, we obtain

\displaystyle S_{0}(X)

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{4}\frac{1}{X^{\frac{7}{4}}}\sum_{R\nearrow X}\frac{1}{R^{\frac{1}{4}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
|\mathbf{x}|\sim X,~|L_{i_{1}}(\mathbf{x})|\sim R\\
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\end{subarray}}\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})^{\varepsilon}L(1,\chi_{Q_{\mathbf{x}}}).

Recalling
(
5.26
),
an application of Lemma
5.10
with
\delta=\frac{1}{16}
and
S=X
, therefore yields

\displaystyle S_{0}(X)

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{X^{\frac{7}{4}}}\sum_{R\nearrow X}\frac{1}{R^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(XR+X^{\frac{33}{32}+\varepsilon}R^{\frac{1}{32}}+X^{\frac{11}{16}+\varepsilon}R^{\frac{5}{4}}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{1}{X^{\frac{7}{4}}}\left(X^{\frac{7}{4}}+X^{\varepsilon}(X^{\frac{27}{16}}+X^{\frac{33}{32}})\right).

Thus
S_{0}(X)=O(1)
on fixing
\varepsilon
to be small enough. This establishes (
7.11
), thereby completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

We are now ready to deduce Proposition
7.1
.
Lemma
7.4
implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{B^{2\eta}\ll X\ll B^{\frac{1}{4}-4\eta}}L_{w_{0}}(B,X)

\displaystyle=\frac{B}{\zeta(2)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\\
B^{2\eta}\ll|\mathbf{x}|\ll B^{\frac{1}{4}-4\eta}\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\infty}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|}

\displaystyle\quad+O(\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}B\log B)+O_{\eta}(B^{1-\frac{1}{16}\eta^{2}}\log B).

We may clearly take
\log B=O(B^{\frac{1}{32}\eta^{2}})
in the second error term.
Moreover, on recalling (
7.11
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{B}{\zeta(2)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\\
B^{\frac{1}{4}-4\eta}\ll|\mathbf{x}|\ll B^{\frac{1}{4}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\infty}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|}

\displaystyle\ll B\sum_{B^{\frac{1}{4}-4\eta}\ll X\ll B^{\frac{1}{4}}}S_{0}(X)\ll\eta B\log B.

Moreover, with further recourse to (
7.11
), it also follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{B^{2\eta}\ll X\ll B^{\frac{1}{4}-4\eta}}L_{w_{0}}(B,X)

\displaystyle=\frac{B}{\zeta(2)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\\
B^{2\eta}\leqslant|\mathbf{x}|\leqslant B^{\frac{1}{4}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\infty}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|}+O(\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}B\log B)+O_{\eta}(B^{1-\frac{1}{32}\eta^{2}}).

Finally, it follows from Theorem
2.1
that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\ll B^{2\eta}\text{ or}\\
B^{\frac{1}{4}-4\eta}\ll X\ll B^{\frac{1}{4}}\end{subarray}}L_{w_{0}}(B,X)\ll\eta B\log B.

This therefore finishes the proof of Proposition
7.1
.

7.2.
Proof of Proposition
7.2
: preliminaries

In this section we are concerned with the asymptotic behaviour of the term

(7.12)

M(B)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}\\
B^{2\eta}\leqslant|\mathbf{x}|\leqslant B^{\frac{1}{4}}\\
\prod_{i=1}^{4}L_{i}(\mathbf{x})\neq\square\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\infty}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|}.

The line of attack follows
[
6
, §6.2]
, but we face extra difficulties that are similar to the ones we encountered in §
5.1
. The basic idea is to restrict each series
\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})
to a sum over small moduli, before interchanging the order of summation. To achieve this, it will be crucial to achieve sufficient cancellation when averaging over the
\mathbf{x}
-sum, which is harder in this setting, since
we have half the number of
\mathbf{x}
-variables compared to
the variety (
1.6
) considered in

[
6
]
.

For
\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}
and
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we let

S_{q}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\bmod{q}\\
\gcd(a,q)=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in({\mathbb{Z}}/q{\mathbb{Z}})^{4}}\operatorname{e}_{q}\left(a\sum_{i=1}^{4}L_{i}(\mathbf{x})b_{i}^{2}\right).

This is multiplicative in
q
and we have

\mathfrak{S}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}q^{-4}S_{q}(\mathbf{x}).

It will be useful to collect together some estimates for
S_{q}(\mathbf{x})
that can be extracted from
[
6
, §4.2]
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{prim}}^{2}
and let
q\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the following estimates hold for any
\varepsilon>0
.

(i)

S_{q}(\mathbf{x})\ll q^{3}\gcd(q,\varDelta(\mathbf{x}))^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(ii)

If
p\nmid 2\varDelta(\mathbf{x})
and
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then

S_{p^{r}}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\frac{\varDelta(\mathbf{x})}{p^{r}}\right)p^{3r}\varphi^{*}(p^{r}),

where
\varphi^{*}(n)=\varphi(n)/n
. Moreover,

S_{p^{r}}(\mathbf{x})=0

if
p\mid\varDelta(\mathbf{x})
but
p\nmid 2\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})
.

(iii)

We have

\sum_{q\leqslant X}\frac{S_{q}(\mathbf{x})}{q^{3}}\ll_{\varepsilon}|\varDelta(\mathbf{x})|^{\frac{3}{16}+\varepsilon}\varDelta_{\operatorname{bad}}(\mathbf{x})^{\frac{3}{8}}X^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon},

if
\varDelta(\mathbf{x})\neq\square
.

(iv)

We have

\sum_{q\leqslant X}\frac{|S_{q}(\mathbf{x})|}{q^{4}}\ll_{\varepsilon}X^{\varepsilon}.

Proof.

It follows from
[
6
, Lemma 4.5]
that
S_{q}(\mathbf{x})\ll q^{3}\prod_{i=1}^{4}\gcd(q,L_{i}(\mathbf{x}))^{\frac{1}{2}}.

But then part (i) is a consequence of the observation (
5.9
) and the fact that
\mathbf{x}
is primitive.
The formulae in (ii) follow from
[
6
, Lemmas 4.6 and 4.8]
.
The case
j=3
